<dd> Set waveform shape the <font SIZE="-1">LFO</font> will use. Can be one of: sine, triangle, square, sawup or sawdown. Default is sine.</p>
<dt><code>amount</code></p>
<dd> Set modulation. Define how much of original signal is affected by the <font SIZE="-1">LFO.</font></p>
<dt><code>offset_l</code></p>
<dd> Set left channel offset. Default is 0. Allowed range is [0 &#8211; 1].</p>
<dt><code>offset_r</code></p>
<dd> Set right channel offset. Default is 0.5. Allowed range is [0 &#8211; 1].</p>
<dt><code>width</code></p>
<dd> Set pulse width. Default is 1. Allowed range is [0 &#8211; 2].</p>
<dt><code>timing</code></p>
<dd> Set possible timing mode. Can be one of: bpm, ms or hz. Default is hz.</p>
<dt><code>bpm</code></p>
<dd> Set bpm. Default is 120. Allowed range is [30 &#8211; 300]. Only used if timing is set to bpm.</p>
<dt><code>ms</code></p>
<dd> Set ms. Default is 500. Allowed range is [10 &#8211; 2000]. Only used if timing is set to ms.</p>
<dt><code>hz</code></p>
<dd> Set frequency in Hz. Default is 2. Allowed range is [0.01 &#8211; 100]. Only used if timing is set to hz.</dl>
<h3>aresample</h3>
<p> Resample the input audio to the specified parameters, using the libswresample library. If none are specified then the filter will automatically convert between its input and output. </p>
<p> This filter is also able to stretch/squeeze the audio data to make it match the timestamps or to inject silence / cut out audio to make it match the timestamps, do a combination of both or do neither. </p>
<p> The filter accepts the syntax [<i>sample_rate</i>:]<i>resampler_options</i>, where <i>sample_rate</i> expresses a sample rate and <i>resampler_options</i> is a list of <i>key</i>=<i>value</i> pairs, separated by &#8220;:&#8221;. See the <code>&#8220;Resampler Options&#8221; section in the <a href="/docs/linux/man/1-ffmpeg-resampler/">ffmpeg-resampler</a>(1) manual</code> for the complete list of supported options. </p>
<p> <i>Examples</i></p>
<dl>
<dt>&bull;</p>
<dd> Resample the input audio to 44100Hz: </p>
<pre>
        aresample=44100
</pre>
<dt>&bull;</p>
<dd> Stretch/squeeze samples to the given timestamps, with a maximum of 1000 samples per second compensation: </p>
<pre>
        aresample=async=1000
</pre>
</dl>
<h3>areverse</h3>
<p> Reverse an audio clip. </p>
<p> Warning: This filter requires memory to buffer the entire clip, so trimming is suggested. </p>
<p> <i>Examples</i></p>
<dl>
<dt>&bull;</p>
<dd> Take the first 5 seconds of a clip, and reverse it. </p>
<pre>
        atrim=end=5,areverse
</pre>
</dl>
<h3>arnndn</h3>
<p> Reduce noise from speech using Recurrent Neural Networks. </p>
<p> This filter accepts the following options:</p>
<dl>
<dt><code>model, m</code></p>
<dd> Set train model file to load. This option is always required.</p>
<dt><code>mix</code></p>
<dd> Set how much to mix filtered samples into final output. Allowed range is from -1 to 1. Default value is 1. Negative values are special, they set how much to keep filtered noise in the final filter output. Set this option to -1 to hear actual noise removed from input signal.</dl>
<p> <i>Commands</i> </p>
<p> This filter supports the all above options as <code>commands</code>.</p>
<h3>asetnsamples</h3>
<p> Set the number of samples per each output audio frame. </p>
<p> The last output packet may contain a different number of samples, as the filter will flush all the remaining samples when the input audio signals its end. </p>
<p> The filter accepts the following options:</p>
<dl>
<dt><code>nb_out_samples, n</code></p>
<dd> Set the number of frames per each output audio frame. The number is intended as the number of samples <i>per each channel</i>. Default value is 1024.</p>
<dt><code>pad, p</code></p>
<dd> If set to 1, the filter will pad the last audio frame with zeroes, so that the last frame will contain the same number of samples as the previous ones. Default value is 1.</dl>
<p> For example, to set the number of per-frame samples to 1234 and disable padding for the last frame, use: </p>
<pre>
        asetnsamples=n=1234:p=0
</pre>
<h3>asetrate</h3>
<p> Set the sample rate without altering the <font SIZE="-1">PCM</font> data. This will result in a change of speed and pitch. </p>
<p> The filter accepts the following options:</p>
<dl>
<dt><code>sample_rate, r</code></p>
<dd> Set the output sample rate. Default is 44100 Hz.</dl>
<h3>ashowinfo</h3>
<p> Show a line containing various information for each input audio frame. The input audio is not modified. </p>
<p> The shown line contains a sequence of key/value pairs of the form <i>key</i>:<i>value</i>. </p>
<p> The following values are shown in the output:</p>
<dl>
<dt><code>n</code></p>
<dd> The (sequential) number of the input frame, starting from 0.</p>
<dt><code>pts</code></p>
<dd> The presentation timestamp of the input frame, in time base units; the time base depends on the filter input pad, and is usually 1/<i>sample_rate</i>.</p>
<dt><code>pts_time</code></p>
<dd> The presentation timestamp of the input frame in seconds.</p>
<dt><code>pos</code></p>
<dd> position of the frame in the input stream, -1 if this information in unavailable and/or meaningless (for example in case of synthetic audio)</p>
<dt><code>fmt</code></p>
<dd> The sample format.</p>
<dt><code>chlayout</code></p>
<dd> The channel layout.</p>
<dt><code>rate</code></p>
<dd> The sample rate for the audio frame.</p>
<dt><code>nb_samples</code></p>
<dd> The number of samples (per channel) in the frame.</p>
<dt><code>checksum</code></p>
<dd> The Adler-32 checksum (printed in hexadecimal) of the audio data. For planar audio, the data is treated as if all the planes were concatenated.</p>
<dt><code>plane_checksums</code></p>
<dd> A list of Adler-32 checksums for each data plane.</dl>
<h3>asoftclip</h3>
<p> Apply audio soft clipping. </p>
<p> Soft clipping is a type of distortion effect where the amplitude of a signal is saturated along a smooth curve, rather than the abrupt shape of hard-clipping. </p>
<p> This filter accepts the following options:</p>
<dl>
<dt><code>type</code></p>
<dd> Set type of soft-clipping. </p>
<p> It accepts the following values:</p>
<dl>
<dt>
<dd>
<dl>
<dt><code>hard</code></p>
<dd>
<dt><code>tanh</code></p>
<dd>
<dt><code>atan</code></p>
<dd>
<dt><code>cubic</code></p>
<dd>
<dt><code>exp</code></p>
<dd>
<dt><code>alg</code></p>
<dd>
<dt><code>quintic</code></p>
<dd>
<dt><code>sin</code></p>
<dd>
<dt><code>erf</code></p>
<dd></dl>
</dl>
<dl>
<dt>
<dd></dl>
<dt><code>threshold</code></p>
<dd> Set threshold from where to start clipping. Default value is 0dB or 1.</p>
<dt><code>output</code></p>
<dd> Set gain applied to output. Default value is 0dB or 1.</p>
<dt><code>param</code></p>
<dd> Set additional parameter which controls sigmoid function.</p>
<dt><code>oversample</code></p>
<dd> Set oversampling factor.</dl>
<p> <i>Commands</i> </p>
<p> This filter supports the all above options as <code>commands</code>.</p>
<h3>asr</h3>
<p> Automatic Speech Recognition </p>
<p> This filter uses PocketSphinx for speech recognition. To enable compilation of this filter, you need to configure FFmpeg with <tt>&quot;--enable-pocketsphinx&quot;</tt>. </p>
<p> It accepts the following options:</p>
<dl>
<dt><code>rate</code></p>
<dd> Set sampling rate of input audio. Defaults is <tt>16000</tt>. This need to match speech models, otherwise one will get poor results.</p>
<dt><code>hmm</code></p>
<dd> Set dictionary containing acoustic model files.</p>
<dt><code>dict</code></p>
<dd> Set pronunciation dictionary.</p>
<dt><code>lm</code></p>
<dd> Set language model file.</p>
<dt><code>lmctl</code></p>
<dd> Set language model set.</p>
<dt><code>lmname</code></p>
<dd> Set which language model to use.</p>
<dt><code>logfn</code></p>
<dd> Set output for log messages.</dl>
<p> The filter exports recognized speech as the frame metadata <tt>&quot;lavfi.asr.text&quot;</tt>.</p>
<h3>astats</h3>
<p> Display time domain statistical information about the audio channels. Statistics are calculated and displayed for each audio channel and, where applicable, an overall figure is also given. </p>
<p> It accepts the following option:</p>
<dl>
<dt><code>length</code></p>
<dd> Short window length in seconds, used for peak and trough <font SIZE="-1">RMS</font> measurement. Default is <tt>0.05</tt> (50 milliseconds). Allowed range is <tt>&quot;[0.01 - 10]&quot;</tt>.</p>
<dt><code>metadata</code></p>
<dd> Set metadata injection. All the metadata keys are prefixed with <tt>&quot;lavfi.astats.X&quot;</tt>, where <tt>&quot;X&quot;</tt> is channel number starting from 1 or string <tt>&quot;Overall&quot;</tt>. Default is disabled. </p>
<p> Available keys for each channel are: DC_offset Min_level Max_level Min_difference Max_difference Mean_difference RMS_difference Peak_level RMS_peak RMS_trough Crest_factor Flat_factor Peak_count Noise_floor Noise_floor_count Bit_depth Dynamic_range Zero_crossings Zero_crossings_rate Number_of_NaNs Number_of_Infs Number_of_denormals </p>
<p> and for Overall: DC_offset Min_level Max_level Min_difference Max_difference Mean_difference RMS_difference Peak_level RMS_level RMS_peak RMS_trough Flat_factor Peak_count Noise_floor Noise_floor_count Bit_depth Number_of_samples Number_of_NaNs Number_of_Infs Number_of_denormals </p>
<p> For example full key look like this <tt>&quot;lavfi.astats.1.DC_offset&quot;</tt> or this <tt>&quot;lavfi.astats.Overall.Peak_count&quot;</tt>. </p>
<p> For description what each key means read below.</p>
<dt><code>reset</code></p>
<dd> Set number of frame after which stats are going to be recalculated. Default is disabled.</p>
<dt><code>measure_perchannel</code></p>
<dd> Select the entries which need to be measured per channel. The metadata keys can be used as flags, default is <code>all</code> which measures everything. <code>none</code> disables all per channel measurement.</p>
<dt><code>measure_overall</code></p>
<dd> Select the entries which need to be measured overall. The metadata keys can be used as flags, default is <code>all</code> which measures everything. <code>none</code> disables all overall measurement.</dl>
<p> A description of each shown parameter follows:</p>
<dl>
<dt><font SIZE="-1"><code>DC</code></font><code> offset</code></p>
<dd> Mean amplitude displacement from zero.</p>
<dt><code>Min level</code></p>
<dd> Minimal sample level.</p>
<dt><code>Max level</code></p>
<dd> Maximal sample level.</p>
<dt><code>Min difference</code></p>
<dd> Minimal difference between two consecutive samples.</p>
<dt><code>Max difference</code></p>
<dd> Maximal difference between two consecutive samples.</p>
<dt><code>Mean difference</code></p>
<dd> Mean difference between two consecutive samples. The average of each difference between two consecutive samples.</p>
<dt><font SIZE="-1"><code>RMS</code></font><code> difference</code></p>
<dd> Root Mean Square difference between two consecutive samples.</p>
<dt><code>Peak level dB</code></p>
<dd>
<dt><font SIZE="-1"><code>RMS</code></font><code> level dB</code></p>
<dd> Standard peak and <font SIZE="-1">RMS</font> level measured in dBFS.</p>
<dt><font SIZE="-1"><code>RMS</code></font><code> peak dB</code></p>
<dd>
<dt><font SIZE="-1"><code>RMS</code></font><code> trough dB</code></p>
<dd> Peak and trough values for <font SIZE="-1">RMS</font> level measured over a short window.</p>
<dt><code>Crest factor</code></p>
<dd> Standard ratio of peak to <font SIZE="-1">RMS</font> level (note: not in dB).</p>
<dt><code>Flat factor</code></p>
<dd> Flatness (i.e. consecutive samples with the same value) of the signal at its peak levels (i.e. either <i>Min level</i> or <i>Max level</i>).</p>
<dt><code>Peak count</code></p>
<dd> Number of occasions (not the number of samples) that the signal attained either <i>Min level</i> or <i>Max level</i>.</p>
<dt><code>Noise floor dB</code></p>
<dd> Minimum local peak measured in dBFS over a short window.</p>
<dt><code>Noise floor count</code></p>
<dd> Number of occasions (not the number of samples) that the signal attained <i>Noise floor</i>.</p>
<dt><code>Bit depth</code></p>
<dd> Overall bit depth of audio. Number of bits used for each sample.</p>
<dt><code>Dynamic range</code></p>
<dd> Measured dynamic range of audio in dB.</p>
<dt><code>Zero crossings</code></p>
<dd> Number of points where the waveform crosses the zero level axis.</p>
<dt><code>Zero crossings rate</code></p>
<dd> Rate of Zero crossings and number of audio samples.</dl>
<h3>asubboost</h3>
<p> Boost subwoofer frequencies. </p>
<p> The filter accepts the following options:</p>
<dl>
<dt><code>dry</code></p>
<dd> Set dry gain, how much of original signal is kept. Allowed range is from 0 to 1. Default value is 0.7.</p>
<dt><code>wet</code></p>
<dd> Set wet gain, how much of filtered signal is kept. Allowed range is from 0 to 1. Default value is 0.7.</p>
<dt><code>decay</code></p>
<dd> Set delay line decay gain value. Allowed range is from 0 to 1. Default value is 0.7.</p>
<dt><code>feedback</code></p>
<dd> Set delay line feedback gain value. Allowed range is from 0 to 1. Default value is 0.9.</p>
<dt><code>cutoff</code></p>
<dd> Set cutoff frequency in Hertz. Allowed range is 50 to 900. Default value is 100.</p>
<dt><code>slope</code></p>
<dd> Set slope amount for cutoff frequency. Allowed range is 0.0001 to 1. Default value is 0.5.</p>
<dt><code>delay</code></p>
<dd> Set delay. Allowed range is from 1 to 100. Default value is 20.</dl>
<p> <i>Commands</i> </p>
<p> This filter supports the all above options as <code>commands</code>.</p>
<h3>asubcut</h3>
<p> Cut subwoofer frequencies. </p>
<p> This filter allows to set custom, steeper roll off than highpass filter, and thus is able to more attenuate frequency content in stop-band. </p>
<p> The filter accepts the following options:</p>
<dl>
<dt><code>cutoff</code></p>
<dd> Set cutoff frequency in Hertz. Allowed range is 2 to 200. Default value is 20.</p>
<dt><code>order</code></p>
<dd> Set filter order. Available values are from 3 to 20. Default value is 10.</p>
<dt><code>level</code></p>
<dd> Set input gain level. Allowed range is from 0 to 1. Default value is 1.</dl>
<p> <i>Commands</i> </p>
<p> This filter supports the all above options as <code>commands</code>.</p>
<h3>asupercut</h3>
<p> Cut super frequencies. </p>
<p> The filter accepts the following options:</p>
<dl>
<dt><code>cutoff</code></p>
<dd> Set cutoff frequency in Hertz. Allowed range is 20000 to 192000. Default value is 20000.</p>
<dt><code>order</code></p>
<dd> Set filter order. Available values are from 3 to 20. Default value is 10.</p>
<dt><code>level</code></p>
<dd> Set input gain level. Allowed range is from 0 to 1. Default value is 1.</dl>
<p> <i>Commands</i> </p>
<p> This filter supports the all above options as <code>commands</code>.</p>
<h3>asuperpass</h3>
<p> Apply high order Butterworth band-pass filter. </p>
<p> The filter accepts the following options:</p>
<dl>
<dt><code>centerf</code></p>
<dd> Set center frequency in Hertz. Allowed range is 2 to 999999. Default value is 1000.</p>
<dt><code>order</code></p>
<dd> Set filter order. Available values are from 4 to 20. Default value is 4.</p>
<dt><code>qfactor</code></p>
<dd> Set Q-factor. Allowed range is from 0.01 to 100. Default value is 1.</p>
<dt><code>level</code></p>
<dd> Set input gain level. Allowed range is from 0 to 2. Default value is 1.</dl>
<p> <i>Commands</i> </p>
<p> This filter supports the all above options as <code>commands</code>.</p>
<h3>asuperstop</h3>
<p> Apply high order Butterworth band-stop filter. </p>
<p> The filter accepts the following options:</p>
<dl>
<dt><code>centerf</code></p>
<dd> Set center frequency in Hertz. Allowed range is 2 to 999999. Default value is 1000.</p>
<dt><code>order</code></p>
<dd> Set filter order. Available values are from 4 to 20. Default value is 4.</p>
<dt><code>qfactor</code></p>
<dd> Set Q-factor. Allowed range is from 0.01 to 100. Default value is 1.</p>
<dt><code>level</code></p>
<dd> Set input gain level. Allowed range is from 0 to 2. Default value is 1.</dl>
<p> <i>Commands</i> </p>
<p> This filter supports the all above options as <code>commands</code>.</p>
<h3>atempo</h3>
<p> Adjust audio tempo. </p>
<p> The filter accepts exactly one parameter, the audio tempo. If not specified then the filter will assume nominal 1.0 tempo. Tempo must be in the [0.5, 100.0] range. </p>
<p> Note that tempo greater than 2 will skip some samples rather than blend them in. If for any reason this is a concern it is always possible to daisy-chain several instances of atempo to achieve the desired product tempo. </p>
<p> <i>Examples</i></p>
<dl>
<dt>&bull;</p>
<dd> Slow down audio to 80% tempo: </p>
<pre>
        atempo=0.8
</pre>
<dt>&bull;</p>
<dd> To speed up audio to 300% tempo: </p>
<pre>
        atempo=3
</pre>
<dt>&bull;</p>
<dd> To speed up audio to 300% tempo by daisy-chaining two atempo instances: </p>
<pre>
        atempo=<a href="//www.systutorials.com/docs/linux/man/3-sqrt/">sqrt</a>(3),atempo=<a href="//www.systutorials.com/docs/linux/man/3-sqrt/">sqrt</a>(3)
</pre>
</dl>
<p> <i>Commands</i> </p>
<p> This filter supports the following commands:</p>
<dl>
<dt><code>tempo</code></p>
<dd> Change filter tempo scale factor. Syntax for the command is : &quot;<i>tempo</i>&quot;</dl>
<h3>atrim</h3>
<p> Trim the input so that the output contains one continuous subpart of the input. </p>
<p> It accepts the following parameters:</p>
<dl>
<dt><code>start</code></p>
<dd> Timestamp (in seconds) of the start of the section to keep. I.e. the audio sample with the timestamp <i>start</i> will be the first sample in the output.</p>
<dt><code>end</code></p>
<dd> Specify time of the first audio sample that will be dropped, i.e. the audio sample immediately preceding the one with the timestamp <i>end</i> will be the last sample in the output.</p>
<dt><code>start_pts</code></p>
<dd> Same as <i>start</i>, except this option sets the start timestamp in samples instead of seconds.</p>
<dt><code>end_pts</code></p>
<dd> Same as <i>end</i>, except this option sets the end timestamp in samples instead of seconds.</p>
<dt><code>duration</code></p>
<dd> The maximum duration of the output in seconds.</p>
<dt><code>start_sample</code></p>
<dd> The number of the first sample that should be output.</p>
<dt><code>end_sample</code></p>
<dd> The number of the first sample that should be dropped.</dl>
<p> <code>start</code>, <code>end</code>, and <code>duration</code> are expressed as time duration specifications; see <code>the Time duration section in the <a href="/docs/linux/man/1-ffmpeg-utils/">ffmpeg-utils</a>(1) manual</code>. </p>
<p> Note that the first two sets of the start/end options and the <code>duration</code> option look at the frame timestamp, while the _sample options simply count the samples that pass through the filter. So start/end_pts and start/end_sample will give different results when the timestamps are wrong, inexact or do not start at zero. Also note that this filter does not modify the timestamps. If you wish to have the output timestamps start at zero, insert the asetpts filter after the atrim filter. </p>
<p> If multiple start or end options are set, this filter tries to be greedy and keep all samples that match at least one of the specified constraints. To keep only the part that matches all the constraints at once, chain multiple atrim filters. </p>
<p> The defaults are such that all the input is kept. So it is possible to set e.g. just the end values to keep everything before the specified time. </p>
<p> Examples:</p>
<dl>
<dt>&bull;</p>
<dd> Drop everything except the second minute of input: </p>
<pre>
        ffmpeg -i INPUT -af atrim=60:120
</pre>
<dt>&bull;</p>
<dd> Keep only the first 1000 samples: </p>
<pre>
        ffmpeg -i INPUT -af atrim=end_sample=1000
</pre>
</dl>
<h3>axcorrelate</h3>
<p> Calculate normalized cross-correlation between two input audio streams. </p>
<p> Resulted samples are always between -1 and 1 inclusive. If result is 1 it means two input samples are highly correlated in that selected segment. Result 0 means they are not correlated at all. If result is -1 it means two input samples are out of phase, which means they cancel each other. </p>
<p> The filter accepts the following options:</p>
<dl>
<dt><code>size</code></p>
<dd> Set size of segment over which cross-correlation is calculated. Default is 256. Allowed range is from 2 to 131072.</p>
<dt><code>algo</code></p>
<dd> Set algorithm for cross-correlation. Can be <tt>&quot;slow&quot;</tt> or <tt>&quot;fast&quot;</tt>. Default is <tt>&quot;slow&quot;</tt>. Fast algorithm assumes mean values over any given segment are always zero and thus need much less calculations to make. This is generally not true, but is valid for typical audio streams.</dl>
<p> <i>Examples</i></p>
<dl>
<dt>&bull;</p>
<dd> Calculate correlation between channels in stereo audio stream: </p>
<pre>
        ffmpeg -i stereo.wav -af channelsplit,axcorrelate=size=1024:algo=fast correlation.wav
</pre>
</dl>
<h3>bandpass</h3>
<p> Apply a two-pole Butterworth band-pass filter with central frequency <i>frequency</i>, and (3dB-point) band-width width. The <i>csg</i> option selects a constant skirt gain (peak gain = Q) instead of the default: constant 0dB peak gain. The filter roll off at 6dB per octave (20dB per decade). </p>
<p> The filter accepts the following options:</p>
<dl>
<dt><code>frequency, f</code></p>
<dd> Set the filter&#8217;s central frequency. Default is <tt>3000</tt>.</p>
<dt><code>csg</code></p>
<dd> Constant skirt gain if set to 1. Defaults to 0.</p>
<dt><code>width_type, t</code></p>
<dd> Set method to specify band-width of filter.</p>
<dl>
<dt>
<dd>
<dl>
<dt><code>h</code></p>
<dd> Hz</p>
<dt><code>q</code></p>
<dd> Q-Factor</p>
<dt><code>o</code></p>
<dd> octave</p>
<dt><code>s</code></p>
<dd> slope</p>
<dt><code>k</code></p>
<dd> kHz</dl>
</dl>
<dl>
<dt>
<dd></dl>
<dt><code>width, w</code></p>
<dd> Specify the band-width of a filter in width_type units.</p>
<dt><code>mix, m</code></p>
<dd> How much to use filtered signal in output. Default is 1. Range is between 0 and 1.</p>
<dt><code>channels, c</code></p>
<dd> Specify which channels to filter, by default all available are filtered.</p>
<dt><code>normalize, n</code></p>
<dd> Normalize biquad coefficients, by default is disabled. Enabling it will normalize magnitude response at <font SIZE="-1">DC</font> to 0dB.</p>
<dt><code>transform, a</code></p>
<dd> Set transform type of <font SIZE="-1">IIR</font> filter.</p>
<dl>
<dt>
<dd>
<dl>
<dt><code>di</code></p>
<dd>
<dt><code>dii</code></p>
<dd>
<dt><code>tdii</code></p>
<dd>
<dt><code>latt</code></p>
<dd></dl>
</dl>
<dl>
<dt>
<dd></dl>
<dt><code>precision, r</code></p>
<dd> Set precison of filtering.</p>
<dl>
<dt>
<dd>
<dl>
<dt><code>auto</code></p>
<dd> Pick automatic sample format depending on surround filters.</p>
<dt><code>s16</code></p>
<dd> Always use signed 16-bit.</p>
<dt><code>s32</code></p>
<dd> Always use signed 32-bit.</p>
<dt><code>f32</code></p>
<dd> Always use float 32-bit.</p>
<dt><code>f64</code></p>
<dd> Always use float 64-bit.</dl>
</dl>
<dl>
<dt>
<dd></dl>
</dl>
<p> <i>Commands</i> </p>
<p> This filter supports the following commands:</p>
<dl>
<dt><code>frequency, f</code></p>
<dd> Change bandpass frequency. Syntax for the command is : &quot;<i>frequency</i>&quot;</p>
<dt><code>width_type, t</code></p>
<dd> Change bandpass width_type. Syntax for the command is : &quot;<i>width_type</i>&quot;</p>
<dt><code>width, w</code></p>
<dd> Change bandpass width. Syntax for the command is : &quot;<i>width</i>&quot;</p>
<dt><code>mix, m</code></p>
<dd> Change bandpass mix. Syntax for the command is : &quot;<i>mix</i>&quot;</dl>
<h3>bandreject</h3>
<p> Apply a two-pole Butterworth band-reject filter with central frequency <i>frequency</i>, and (3dB-point) band-width <i>width</i>. The filter roll off at 6dB per octave (20dB per decade). </p>
<p> The filter accepts the following options:</p>
<dl>
<dt><code>frequency, f</code></p>
<dd> Set the filter&#8217;s central frequency. Default is <tt>3000</tt>.</p>
<dt><code>width_type, t</code></p>
<dd> Set method to specify band-width of filter.</p>
<dl>
<dt>
<dd>
<dl>
<dt><code>h</code></p>
<dd> Hz</p>
<dt><code>q</code></p>
<dd> Q-Factor</p>
<dt><code>o</code></p>
<dd> octave</p>
<dt><code>s</code></p>
<dd> slope</p>
<dt><code>k</code></p>
<dd> kHz</dl>
</dl>
<dl>
<dt>
<dd></dl>
<dt><code>width, w</code></p>
<dd> Specify the band-width of a filter in width_type units.</p>
<dt><code>mix, m</code></p>
<dd> How much to use filtered signal in output. Default is 1. Range is between 0 and 1.</p>
<dt><code>channels, c</code></p>
<dd> Specify which channels to filter, by default all available are filtered.</p>
<dt><code>normalize, n</code></p>
<dd> Normalize biquad coefficients, by default is disabled. Enabling it will normalize magnitude response at <font SIZE="-1">DC</font> to 0dB.</p>
<dt><code>transform, a</code></p>
<dd> Set transform type of <font SIZE="-1">IIR</font> filter.</p>
<dl>
<dt>
<dd>
<dl>
<dt><code>di</code></p>
<dd>
<dt><code>dii</code></p>
<dd>
<dt><code>tdii</code></p>
<dd>
<dt><code>latt</code></p>
<dd></dl>
</dl>
<dl>
<dt>
<dd></dl>
<dt><code>precision, r</code></p>
<dd> Set precison of filtering.</p>
<dl>
<dt>
<dd>
<dl>
<dt><code>auto</code></p>
<dd> Pick automatic sample format depending on surround filters.</p>
<dt><code>s16</code></p>
<dd> Always use signed 16-bit.</p>
<dt><code>s32</code></p>
<dd> Always use signed 32-bit.</p>
<dt><code>f32</code></p>
<dd> Always use float 32-bit.</p>
<dt><code>f64</code></p>
<dd> Always use float 64-bit.</dl>
</dl>
<dl>
<dt>
<dd></dl>
</dl>
<p> <i>Commands</i> </p>
<p> This filter supports the following commands:</p>
<dl>
<dt><code>frequency, f</code></p>
<dd> Change bandreject frequency. Syntax for the command is : &quot;<i>frequency</i>&quot;</p>
<dt><code>width_type, t</code></p>
<dd> Change bandreject width_type. Syntax for the command is : &quot;<i>width_type</i>&quot;</p>
<dt><code>width, w</code></p>
<dd> Change bandreject width. Syntax for the command is : &quot;<i>width</i>&quot;</p>
<dt><code>mix, m</code></p>
<dd> Change bandreject mix. Syntax for the command is : &quot;<i>mix</i>&quot;</dl>
<h3>bass, lowshelf</h3>
<p> Boost or cut the bass (lower) frequencies of the audio using a two-pole shelving filter with a response similar to that of a standard hi-fi&#8217;s tone-controls. This is also known as shelving equalisation (<font SIZE="-1">EQ</font>). </p>
<p> The filter accepts the following options:</p>
<dl>
<dt><code>gain, g</code></p>
<dd> Give the gain at 0 Hz. Its useful range is about -20 (for a large cut) to +20 (for a large boost). Beware of clipping when using a positive gain.</p>
<dt><code>frequency, f</code></p>
<dd> Set the filter&#8217;s central frequency and so can be used to extend or reduce the frequency range to be boosted or cut. The default value is <tt>100</tt> Hz.</p>
<dt><code>width_type, t</code></p>
<dd> Set method to specify band-width of filter.</p>
<dl>
<dt>
<dd>
<dl>
<dt><code>h</code></p>
<dd> Hz</p>
<dt><code>q</code></p>
<dd> Q-Factor</p>
<dt><code>o</code></p>
<dd> octave</p>
<dt><code>s</code></p>
<dd> slope</p>
<dt><code>k</code></p>
<dd> kHz</dl>
</dl>
<dl>
<dt>
<dd></dl>
<dt><code>width, w</code></p>
<dd> Determine how steep is the filter&#8217;s shelf transition.</p>
<dt><code>poles, p</code></p>
<dd> Set number of poles. Default is 2.</p>
<dt><code>mix, m</code></p>
<dd> How much to use filtered signal in output. Default is 1. Range is between 0 and 1.</p>
<dt><code>channels, c</code></p>
<dd> Specify which channels to filter, by default all available are filtered.</p>
<dt><code>normalize, n</code></p>
<dd> Normalize biquad coefficients, by default is disabled. Enabling it will normalize magnitude response at <font SIZE="-1">DC</font> to 0dB.</p>
<dt><code>transform, a</code></p>
<dd> Set transform type of <font SIZE="-1">IIR</font> filter.</p>
<dl>
<dt>
<dd>
<dl>
<dt><code>di</code></p>
<dd>
<dt><code>dii</code></p>
<dd>
<dt><code>tdii</code></p>
<dd>
<dt><code>latt</code></p>
<dd></dl>
</dl>
<dl>
<dt>
<dd></dl>
<dt><code>precision, r</code></p>
<dd> Set precison of filtering.</p>
<dl>
<dt>
<dd>
<dl>
<dt><code>auto</code></p>
<dd> Pick automatic sample format depending on surround filters.</p>
<dt><code>s16</code></p>
<dd> Always use signed 16-bit.</p>
<dt><code>s32</code></p>
<dd> Always use signed 32-bit.</p>
<dt><code>f32</code></p>
<dd> Always use float 32-bit.</p>
<dt><code>f64</code></p>
<dd> Always use float 64-bit.</dl>
</dl>
<dl>
<dt>
<dd></dl>
</dl>
<p> <i>Commands</i> </p>
<p> This filter supports the following commands:</p>
<dl>
<dt><code>frequency, f</code></p>
<dd> Change bass frequency. Syntax for the command is : &quot;<i>frequency</i>&quot;</p>
<dt><code>width_type, t</code></p>
<dd> Change bass width_type. Syntax for the command is : &quot;<i>width_type</i>&quot;</p>
<dt><code>width, w</code></p>
<dd> Change bass width. Syntax for the command is : &quot;<i>width</i>&quot;</p>
<dt><code>gain, g</code></p>
<dd> Change bass gain. Syntax for the command is : &quot;<i>gain</i>&quot;</p>
<dt><code>mix, m</code></p>
<dd> Change bass mix. Syntax for the command is : &quot;<i>mix</i>&quot;</dl>
<h3>biquad</h3>
<p> Apply a biquad <font SIZE="-1">IIR</font> filter with the given coefficients. Where <i>b0</i>, <i>b1</i>, <i>b2</i> and <i>a0</i>, <i>a1</i>, <i>a2</i> are the numerator and denominator coefficients respectively. and <i>channels</i>, <i>c</i> specify which channels to filter, by default all available are filtered. </p>
<p> <i>Commands</i> </p>
<p> This filter supports the following commands:</p>
<dl>
<dt><code>a0</code></p>
<dd>
<dt><code>a1</code></p>
<dd>
<dt><code>a2</code></p>
<dd>
<dt><code>b0</code></p>
<dd>
<dt><code>b1</code></p>
<dd>
<dt><code>b2</code></p>
<dd> Change biquad parameter. Syntax for the command is : &quot;<i>value</i>&quot;</p>
<dt><code>mix, m</code></p>
<dd> How much to use filtered signal in output. Default is 1. Range is between 0 and 1.</p>
<dt><code>channels, c</code></p>
<dd> Specify which channels to filter, by default all available are filtered.</p>
<dt><code>normalize, n</code></p>
<dd> Normalize biquad coefficients, by default is disabled. Enabling it will normalize magnitude response at <font SIZE="-1">DC</font> to 0dB.</p>
<dt><code>transform, a</code></p>
<dd> Set transform type of <font SIZE="-1">IIR</font> filter.</p>
<dl>
<dt>
<dd>
<dl>
<dt><code>di</code></p>
<dd>
<dt><code>dii</code></p>
<dd>
<dt><code>tdii</code></p>
<dd>
<dt><code>latt</code></p>
<dd></dl>
</dl>
<dl>
<dt>
<dd></dl>
<dt><code>precision, r</code></p>
<dd> Set precison of filtering.</p>
<dl>
<dt>
<dd>
<dl>
<dt><code>auto</code></p>
<dd> Pick automatic sample format depending on surround filters.</p>
<dt><code>s16</code></p>
<dd> Always use signed 16-bit.</p>
<dt><code>s32</code></p>
<dd> Always use signed 32-bit.</p>
<dt><code>f32</code></p>
<dd> Always use float 32-bit.</p>
<dt><code>f64</code></p>
<dd> Always use float 64-bit.</dl>
</dl>
<dl>
<dt>
<dd></dl>
</dl>
<h3>bs2b</h3>
<p> Bauer stereo to binaural transformation, which improves headphone listening of stereo audio records. </p>
<p> To enable compilation of this filter you need to configure FFmpeg with <tt>&quot;--enable-libbs2b&quot;</tt>. </p>
<p> It accepts the following parameters:</p>
<dl>
<dt><code>profile</code></p>
<dd> Pre-defined crossfeed level.</p>
<dl>
<dt>
<dd>
<dl>
<dt><code>default</code></p>
<dd> Default level (fcut=700, feed=50).</p>
<dt><code>cmoy</code></p>
<dd> Chu Moy circuit (fcut=700, feed=60).</p>
<dt><code>jmeier</code></p>
<dd> Jan Meier circuit (fcut=650, feed=95).</dl>
</dl>
<dl>
<dt>
<dd></dl>
<dt><code>fcut</code></p>
<dd> Cut frequency (in Hz).</p>
<dt><code>feed</code></p>
<dd> Feed level (in Hz).</dl>
<h3>channelmap</h3>
<p> Remap input channels to new locations. </p>
<p> It accepts the following parameters:</p>
<dl>
<dt><code>map</code></p>
<dd> Map channels from input to output. The argument is a &#8216;|&#8217;-separated list of mappings, each in the <tt>&quot;</tt>in_channel<tt>-</tt>out_channel<tt>&quot;</tt> or <i>in_channel</i> form. <i>in_channel</i> can be either the name of the input channel (e.g. <font SIZE="-1">FL</font> for front left) or its index in the input channel layout. <i>out_channel</i> is the name of the output channel or its index in the output channel layout. If <i>out_channel</i> is not given then it is implicitly an index, starting with zero and increasing by one for each mapping.</p>
<dt><code>channel_layout</code></p>
<dd> The channel layout of the output stream.</dl>
<p> If no mapping is present, the filter will implicitly map input channels to output channels, preserving indices. </p>
<p> <i>Examples</i></p>
<dl>
<dt>&bull;</p>
<dd> For example, assuming a 5.1+downmix input <font SIZE="-1">MOV</font> file, </p>
<pre>
        ffmpeg -i in.mov -filter 'channelmap=map=DL-FL|DR-FR' out.wav
</pre>
<p> will create an output <font SIZE="-1">WAV</font> file tagged as stereo from the downmix channels of the input.</p>
<dt>&bull;</p>
<dd> To fix a 5.1 <font SIZE="-1">WAV</font> improperly encoded in <font SIZE="-1">AAC</font>&#8216;s native channel order </p>
<pre>
        ffmpeg -i in.wav -filter 'channelmap=1|2|0|5|3|4:5.1' out.wav
</pre>
</dl>
<h3>channelsplit</h3>
<p> Split each channel from an input audio stream into a separate output stream. </p>
<p> It accepts the following parameters:</p>
<dl>
<dt><code>channel_layout</code></p>
<dd> The channel layout of the input stream. The default is &#8220;stereo&#8221;.</p>
<dt><code>channels</code></p>
<dd> A channel layout describing the channels to be extracted as separate output streams or &#8220;all&#8221; to extract each input channel as a separate stream. The default is &#8220;all&#8221;. </p>
<p> Choosing channels not present in channel layout in the input will result in an error.</dl>
<p> <i>Examples</i></p>
<dl>
<dt>&bull;</p>
<dd> For example, assuming a stereo input <font SIZE="-1">MP3</font> file, </p>
<pre>
        ffmpeg -i in.mp3 -filter_complex channelsplit out.mkv
</pre>
<p> will create an output Matroska file with two audio streams, one containing only the left channel and the other the right channel.</p>
<dt>&bull;</p>
<dd> Split a 5.1 <font SIZE="-1">WAV</font> file into per-channel files: </p>
<p>ffmpeg &#8211; i in.wav &#8211; filter_complex &#8216;channelsplit=channel_layout=5.1[FL][FR][FC][LFE][SL][SR]&#8217; &#8211; map &#8216;[FL]&#8217; front_left.wav &#8211; map &#8216;[FR]&#8217; front_right.wav &#8211; map &#8216;[FC]&#8217; front_center.wav &#8211; map &#8216;[LFE]&#8217; lfe.wav &#8211; map &#8216;[SL]&#8217; side_left.wav &#8211; map &#8216;[SR]&#8217; side_right.wav</p>
<dt>&bull;</p>
<dd> Extract only <font SIZE="-1">LFE</font> from a 5.1 <font SIZE="-1">WAV</font> file: </p>
<p>ffmpeg &#8211; i in.wav &#8211; filter_complex &#8216;channelsplit=channel_layout=5.1:channels=LFE[LFE]&#8217; &#8211; map &#8216;[LFE]&#8217; lfe.wav
</dl>
<h3>chorus</h3>
<p> Add a chorus effect to the audio. </p>
<p> Can make a single vocal sound like a chorus, but can also be applied to instrumentation. </p>
<p> Chorus resembles an echo effect with a short delay, but whereas with echo the delay is constant, with chorus, it is varied using using sinusoidal or triangular modulation. The modulation depth defines the range the modulated delay is played before or after the delay. Hence the delayed sound will sound slower or faster, that is the delayed sound tuned around the original one, like in a chorus where some vocals are slightly off key. </p>
<p> It accepts the following parameters:</p>
<dl>
<dt><code>in_gain</code></p>
<dd> Set input gain. Default is 0.4.</p>
<dt><code>out_gain</code></p>
<dd> Set output gain. Default is 0.4.</p>
<dt><code>delays</code></p>
<dd> Set delays. A typical delay is around 40ms to 60ms.</p>
<dt><code>decays</code></p>
<dd> Set decays.</p>
<dt><code>speeds</code></p>
<dd> Set speeds.</p>
<dt><code>depths</code></p>
<dd> Set depths.</dl>
<p> <i>Examples</i></p>
<dl>
<dt>&bull;</p>
<dd> A single delay: </p>
<pre>
        chorus=0.7:0.9:55:0.4:0.25:2
</pre>
<dt>&bull;</p>
<dd> Two delays: </p>
<pre>
        chorus=0.6:0.9:50|60:0.4|0.32:0.25|0.4:2|1.3
</pre>
<dt>&bull;</p>
<dd> Fuller sounding chorus with three delays: </p>
<pre>
        chorus=0.5:0.9:50|60|40:0.4|0.32|0.3:0.25|0.4|0.3:2|2.3|1.3
</pre>
</dl>
<h3>compand</h3>
<p> Compress or expand the audio&#8217;s dynamic range. </p>
<p> It accepts the following parameters:</p>
<dl>
<dt><code>attacks</code></p>
<dd>
<dt><code>decays</code></p>
<dd> A list of times in seconds for each channel over which the instantaneous level of the input signal is averaged to determine its volume. <i>attacks</i> refers to increase of volume and <i>decays</i> refers to decrease of volume. For most situations, the attack time (response to the audio getting louder) should be shorter than the decay time, because the human ear is more sensitive to sudden loud audio than sudden soft audio. A typical value for attack is 0.3 seconds and a typical value for decay is 0.8 seconds. If specified number of attacks &amp; decays is lower than number of channels, the last set attack/decay will be used for all remaining channels.</p>
<dt><code>points</code></p>
<dd> A list of points for the transfer function, specified in dB relative to the maximum possible signal amplitude. Each key points list must be defined using the following syntax: <tt>&quot;x0/y0|x1/y1|x2/y2|....&quot;</tt> or <tt>&quot;x0/y0 x1/y1 x2/y2 ....&quot;</tt> </p>
<p> The input values must be in strictly increasing order but the transfer function does not have to be monotonically rising. The point <tt>&quot;0/0&quot;</tt> is assumed but may be overridden (by <tt>&quot;0/out-dBn&quot;</tt>). Typical values for the transfer function are <tt>&quot;-70/-70|-60/-20|1/0&quot;</tt>.</p>
<dt><code>soft-knee</code></p>
<dd> Set the curve radius in dB for all joints. It defaults to 0.01.</p>
<dt><code>gain</code></p>
<dd> Set the additional gain in dB to be applied at all points on the transfer function. This allows for easy adjustment of the overall gain. It defaults to 0.</p>
<dt><code>volume</code></p>
<dd> Set an initial volume, in dB, to be assumed for each channel when filtering starts. This permits the user to supply a nominal level initially, so that, for example, a very large gain is not applied to initial signal levels before the companding has begun to operate. A typical value for audio which is initially quiet is -90 dB. It defaults to 0.</p>
<dt><code>delay</code></p>
<dd> Set a delay, in seconds. The input audio is analyzed immediately, but audio is delayed before being fed to the volume adjuster. Specifying a delay approximately equal to the attack/decay times allows the filter to effectively operate in predictive rather than reactive mode. It defaults to 0.</dl>
<p> <i>Examples</i></p>
<dl>
<dt>&bull;</p>
<dd> Make music with both quiet and loud passages suitable for listening to in a noisy environment: </p>
<pre>
        compand=.3|.3:1|1:-90/-60|-60/-40|-40/-30|-20/-20:6:0:-90:0.2
</pre>
<p> Another example for audio with whisper and explosion parts: </p>
<pre>
        compand=0|0:1|1:-90/-900|-70/-70|-30/-9|0/-3:6:0:0:0
</pre>
<dt>&bull;</p>
<dd> A noise gate for when the noise is at a lower level than the signal: </p>
<pre>
        compand=.1|.1:.2|.2:-900/-900|-50.1/-900|-50/-50:.01:0:-90:.1
</pre>
<dt>&bull;</p>
<dd> Here is another noise gate, this time for when the noise is at a higher level than the signal (making it, in some ways, similar to squelch): </p>
<pre>
        compand=.1|.1:.1|.1:-45.1/-45.1|-45/-900|0/-900:.01:45:-90:.1
</pre>
<dt>&bull;</p>
<dd> 2:1 compression starting at -6dB: </p>
<pre>
        compand=points=-80/-80|-6/-6|0/-3.8|20/3.5
</pre>
<dt>&bull;</p>
<dd> 2:1 compression starting at -9dB: </p>
<pre>
        compand=points=-80/-80|-9/-9|0/-5.3|20/2.9
</pre>
<dt>&bull;</p>
<dd> 2:1 compression starting at -12dB: </p>
<pre>
        compand=points=-80/-80|-12/-12|0/-6.8|20/1.9
</pre>
<dt>&bull;</p>
<dd> 2:1 compression starting at -18dB: </p>
<pre>
        compand=points=-80/-80|-18/-18|0/-9.8|20/0.7
</pre>
<dt>&bull;</p>
<dd> 3:1 compression starting at -15dB: </p>
<pre>
        compand=points=-80/-80|-15/-15|0/-10.8|20/-5.2
</pre>
<dt>&bull;</p>
<dd> Compressor/Gate: </p>
<pre>
        compand=points=-80/-105|-62/-80|-15.4/-15.4|0/-12|20/-7.6
</pre>
<dt>&bull;</p>
<dd> Expander: </p>
<pre>
        compand=attacks=0:points=-80/-169|-54/-80|-49.5/-64.6|-41.1/-41.1|-25.8/-15|-10.8/-4.5|0/0|20/8.3
</pre>
<dt>&bull;</p>
<dd> Hard limiter at -6dB: </p>
<pre>
        compand=attacks=0:points=-80/-80|-6/-6|20/-6
</pre>
<dt>&bull;</p>
<dd> Hard limiter at -12dB: </p>
<pre>
        compand=attacks=0:points=-80/-80|-12/-12|20/-12
</pre>
<dt>&bull;</p>
<dd> Hard noise gate at -35 dB: </p>
<pre>
        compand=attacks=0:points=-80/-115|-35.1/-80|-35/-35|20/20
</pre>
<dt>&bull;</p>
<dd> Soft limiter: </p>
<pre>
        compand=attacks=0:points=-80/-80|-12.4/-12.4|-6/-8|0/-6.8|20/-2.8
</pre>
</dl>
<h3>compensationdelay</h3>
<p> Compensation Delay Line is a metric based delay to compensate differing positions of microphones or speakers. </p>
<p> For example, you have recorded guitar with two microphones placed in different locations. Because the front of sound wave has fixed speed in normal conditions, the phasing of microphones can vary and depends on their location and interposition. The best sound mix can be achieved when these microphones are in phase (synchronized). Note that a distance of ~30 cm between microphones makes one microphone capture the signal in antiphase to the other microphone. That makes the final mix sound moody. This filter helps to solve phasing problems by adding different delays to each microphone track and make them synchronized. </p>
<p> The best result can be reached when you take one track as base and synchronize other tracks one by one with it. Remember that synchronization/delay tolerance depends on sample rate, too. Higher sample rates will give more tolerance. </p>
<p> The filter accepts the following parameters:</p>
<dl>
<dt><code>mm</code></p>
<dd> Set millimeters distance. This is compensation distance for fine tuning. Default is 0.</p>
<dt><code>cm</code></p>
<dd> Set cm distance. This is compensation distance for tightening distance setup. Default is 0.</p>
<dt><code>m</code></p>
<dd> Set meters distance. This is compensation distance for hard distance setup. Default is 0.</p>
<dt><code>dry</code></p>
<dd> Set dry amount. Amount of unprocessed (dry) signal. Default is 0.</p>
<dt><code>wet</code></p>
<dd> Set wet amount. Amount of processed (wet) signal. Default is 1.</p>
<dt><code>temp</code></p>
<dd> Set temperature in degrees Celsius. This is the temperature of the environment. Default is 20.</dl>
<h3>crossfeed</h3>
<p> Apply headphone crossfeed filter. </p>
<p> Crossfeed is the process of blending the left and right channels of stereo audio recording. It is mainly used to reduce extreme stereo separation of low frequencies. </p>
<p> The intent is to produce more speaker like sound to the listener. </p>
<p> The filter accepts the following options:</p>
<dl>
<dt><code>strength</code></p>
<dd> Set strength of crossfeed. Default is 0.2. Allowed range is from 0 to 1. This sets gain of low shelf filter for side part of stereo image. Default is -6dB. Max allowed is -30db when strength is set to 1.</p>
<dt><code>range</code></p>
<dd> Set soundstage wideness. Default is 0.5. Allowed range is from 0 to 1. This sets cut off frequency of low shelf filter. Default is cut off near 1550 Hz. With range set to 1 cut off frequency is set to 2100 Hz.</p>
<dt><code>slope</code></p>
<dd> Set curve slope of low shelf filter. Default is 0.5. Allowed range is from 0.01 to 1.</p>
<dt><code>level_in</code></p>
<dd> Set input gain. Default is 0.9.</p>
<dt><code>level_out</code></p>
<dd> Set output gain. Default is 1.</dl>
<p> <i>Commands</i> </p>
<p> This filter supports the all above options as <code>commands</code>.</p>
<h3>crystalizer</h3>
<p> Simple algorithm for audio noise sharpening. </p>
<p> This filter linearly increases differences betweeen each audio sample. </p>
<p> The filter accepts the following options:</p>
<dl>
<dt><code>i</code></p>
<dd> Sets the intensity of effect (default: 2.0). Must be in range between -10.0 to 0 (unchanged sound) to 10.0 (maximum effect). To inverse filtering use negative value.</p>
<dt><code>c</code></p>
<dd> Enable clipping. By default is enabled.</dl>
<p> <i>Commands</i> </p>
<p> This filter supports the all above options as <code>commands</code>.</p>
<h3>dcshift</h3>
<p> Apply a <font SIZE="-1">DC</font> shift to the audio. </p>
<p> This can be useful to remove a <font SIZE="-1">DC</font> offset (caused perhaps by a hardware problem in the recording chain) from the audio. The effect of a <font SIZE="-1">DC</font> offset is reduced headroom and hence volume. The <code>astats</code> filter can be used to determine if a signal has a <font SIZE="-1">DC</font> offset.</p>
<dl>
<dt><code>shift</code></p>
<dd> Set the <font SIZE="-1">DC</font> shift, allowed range is [-1, 1]. It indicates the amount to shift the audio.</p>
<dt><code>limitergain</code></p>
<dd> Optional. It should have a value much less than 1 (e.g. 0.05 or 0.02) and is used to prevent clipping.</dl>
<h3>deesser</h3>
<p> Apply de-essing to the audio samples.</p>
<dl>
<dt><code>i</code></p>
<dd> Set intensity for triggering de-essing. Allowed range is from 0 to 1. Default is 0.</p>
<dt><code>m</code></p>
<dd> Set amount of ducking on treble part of sound. Allowed range is from 0 to 1. Default is 0.5.</p>
<dt><code>f</code></p>
<dd> How much of original frequency content to keep when de-essing. Allowed range is from 0 to 1. Default is 0.5.</p>
<dt><code>s</code></p>
<dd> Set the output mode. </p>
<p> It accepts the following values:</p>
<dl>
<dt>
<dd>
<dl>
<dt><code>i</code></p>
<dd> Pass input unchanged.</p>
<dt><code>o</code></p>
<dd> Pass ess filtered out.</p>
<dt><code>e</code></p>
<dd> Pass only ess. </p>
<p> Default value is <i>o</i>.</dl>
</dl>
<dl>
<dt>
<dd></dl>
</dl>
<h3>drmeter</h3>
<p> Measure audio dynamic range. </p>
<p> <font SIZE="-1">DR</font> values of 14 and higher is found in very dynamic material. <font SIZE="-1">DR</font> of 8 to 13 is found in transition material. And anything less that 8 have very poor dynamics and is very compressed. </p>
<p> The filter accepts the following options:</p>
<dl>
<dt><code>length</code></p>
<dd> Set window length in seconds used to split audio into segments of equal length. Default is 3 seconds.</dl>
<h3>dynaudnorm</h3>
<p> Dynamic Audio Normalizer. </p>
<p> This filter applies a certain amount of gain to the input audio in order to bring its peak magnitude to a target level (e.g. 0 dBFS). However, in contrast to more &#8220;simple&#8221; normalization algorithms, the Dynamic Audio Normalizer *dynamically* re-adjusts the gain factor to the input audio. This allows for applying extra gain to the &#8220;quiet&#8221; sections of the audio while avoiding distortions or clipping the &#8220;loud&#8221; sections. In other words: The Dynamic Audio Normalizer will &#8220;even out&#8221; the volume of quiet and loud sections, in the sense that the volume of each section is brought to the same target level. Note, however, that the Dynamic Audio Normalizer achieves this goal *without* applying &#8220;dynamic range compressing&#8221;. It will retain 100% of the dynamic range *within* each section of the audio file.</p>
<dl>
<dt><code>framelen, f</code></p>
<dd> Set the frame length in milliseconds. In range from 10 to 8000 milliseconds. Default is 500 milliseconds. The Dynamic Audio Normalizer processes the input audio in small chunks, referred to as frames. This is required, because a peak magnitude has no meaning for just a single sample value. Instead, we need to determine the peak magnitude for a contiguous sequence of sample values. While a &#8220;standard&#8221; normalizer would simply use the peak magnitude of the complete file, the Dynamic Audio Normalizer determines the peak magnitude individually for each frame. The length of a frame is specified in milliseconds. By default, the Dynamic Audio Normalizer uses a frame length of 500 milliseconds, which has been found to give good results with most files. Note that the exact frame length, in number of samples, will be determined automatically, based on the sampling rate of the individual input audio file.</p>
<dt><code>gausssize, g</code></p>
<dd> Set the Gaussian filter window size. In range from 3 to 301, must be odd number. Default is 31. Probably the most important parameter of the Dynamic Audio Normalizer is the <tt>&quot;window size&quot;</tt> of the Gaussian smoothing filter. The filter&#8217;s window size is specified in frames, centered around the current frame. For the sake of simplicity, this must be an odd number. Consequently, the default value of 31 takes into account the current frame, as well as the 15 preceding frames and the 15 subsequent frames. Using a larger window results in a stronger smoothing effect and thus in less gain variation, i.e. slower gain adaptation. Conversely, using a smaller window results in a weaker smoothing effect and thus in more gain variation, i.e. faster gain adaptation. In other words, the more you increase this value, the more the Dynamic Audio Normalizer will behave like a &#8220;traditional&#8221; normalization filter. On the contrary, the more you decrease this value, the more the Dynamic Audio Normalizer will behave like a dynamic range compressor.</p>
<dt><code>peak, p</code></p>
<dd> Set the target peak value. This specifies the highest permissible magnitude level for the normalized audio input. This filter will try to approach the target peak magnitude as closely as possible, but at the same time it also makes sure that the normalized signal will never exceed the peak magnitude. A frame&#8217;s maximum local gain factor is imposed directly by the target peak magnitude. The default value is 0.95 and thus leaves a headroom of 5%*. It is not recommended to go above this value.</p>
<dt><code>maxgain, m</code></p>
<dd> Set the maximum gain factor. In range from 1.0 to 100.0. Default is 10.0. The Dynamic Audio Normalizer determines the maximum possible (local) gain factor for each input frame, i.e. the maximum gain factor that does not result in clipping or distortion. The maximum gain factor is determined by the frame&#8217;s highest magnitude sample. However, the Dynamic Audio Normalizer additionally bounds the frame&#8217;s maximum gain factor by a predetermined (global) maximum gain factor. This is done in order to avoid excessive gain factors in &#8220;silent&#8221; or almost silent frames. By default, the maximum gain factor is 10.0, For most inputs the default value should be sufficient and it usually is not recommended to increase this value. Though, for input with an extremely low overall volume level, it may be necessary to allow even higher gain factors. Note, however, that the Dynamic Audio Normalizer does not simply apply a &#8220;hard&#8221; threshold (i.e. cut off values above the threshold). Instead, a &#8220;sigmoid&#8221; threshold function will be applied. This way, the gain factors will smoothly approach the threshold value, but never exceed that value.</p>
<dt><code>targetrms, r</code></p>
<dd> Set the target <font SIZE="-1">RMS.</font> In range from 0.0 to 1.0. Default is 0.0 &#8211; disabled. By default, the Dynamic Audio Normalizer performs &#8220;peak&#8221; normalization. This means that the maximum local gain factor for each frame is defined (only) by the frame&#8217;s highest magnitude sample. This way, the samples can be amplified as much as possible without exceeding the maximum signal level, i.e. without clipping. Optionally, however, the Dynamic Audio Normalizer can also take into account the frame&#8217;s root mean square, abbreviated <font SIZE="-1">RMS.</font> In electrical engineering, the <font SIZE="-1">RMS</font> is commonly used to determine the power of a time-varying signal. It is therefore considered that the <font SIZE="-1">RMS</font> is a better approximation of the &#8220;perceived loudness&#8221; than just looking at the signal&#8217;s peak magnitude. Consequently, by adjusting all frames to a constant <font SIZE="-1">RMS</font> value, a uniform &#8220;perceived loudness&#8221; can be established. If a target <font SIZE="-1">RMS</font> value has been specified, a frame&#8217;s local gain factor is defined as the factor that would result in exactly that <font SIZE="-1">RMS</font> value. Note, however, that the maximum local gain factor is still restricted by the frame&#8217;s highest magnitude sample, in order to prevent clipping.</p>
<dt><code>coupling, n</code></p>
<dd> Enable channels coupling. By default is enabled. By default, the Dynamic Audio Normalizer will amplify all channels by the same amount. This means the same gain factor will be applied to all channels, i.e. the maximum possible gain factor is determined by the &#8220;loudest&#8221; channel. However, in some recordings, it may happen that the volume of the different channels is uneven, e.g. one channel may be &#8220;quieter&#8221; than the other one(s). In this case, this option can be used to disable the channel coupling. This way, the gain factor will be determined independently for each channel, depending only on the individual channel&#8217;s highest magnitude sample. This allows for harmonizing the volume of the different channels.</p>
<dt><code>correctdc, c</code></p>
<dd> Enable <font SIZE="-1">DC</font> bias correction. By default is disabled. An audio signal (in the time domain) is a sequence of sample values. In the Dynamic Audio Normalizer these sample values are represented in the -1.0 to 1.0 range, regardless of the original input format. Normally, the audio signal, or &#8220;waveform&#8221;, should be centered around the zero point. That means if we calculate the mean value of all samples in a file, or in a single frame, then the result should be 0.0 or at least very close to that value. If, however, there is a significant deviation of the mean value from 0.0, in either positive or negative direction, this is referred to as a <font SIZE="-1">DC</font> bias or <font SIZE="-1">DC</font> offset. Since a <font SIZE="-1">DC</font> bias is clearly undesirable, the Dynamic Audio Normalizer provides optional <font SIZE="-1">DC</font> bias correction. With <font SIZE="-1">DC</font> bias correction enabled, the Dynamic Audio Normalizer will determine the mean value, or &#8220;<font SIZE="-1">DC</font> correction&#8221; offset, of each input frame and subtract that value from all of the frame&#8217;s sample values which ensures those samples are centered around 0.0 again. Also, in order to avoid &#8220;gaps&#8221; at the frame boundaries, the <font SIZE="-1">DC</font> correction offset values will be interpolated smoothly between neighbouring frames.</p>
<dt><code>altboundary, b</code></p>
<dd> Enable alternative boundary mode. By default is disabled. The Dynamic Audio Normalizer takes into account a certain neighbourhood around each frame. This includes the preceding frames as well as the subsequent frames. However, for the &#8220;boundary&#8221; frames, located at the very beginning and at the very end of the audio file, not all neighbouring frames are available. In particular, for the first few frames in the audio file, the preceding frames are not known. And, similarly, for the last few frames in the audio file, the subsequent frames are not known. Thus, the question arises which gain factors should be assumed for the missing frames in the &#8220;boundary&#8221; region. The Dynamic Audio Normalizer implements two modes to deal with this situation. The default boundary mode assumes a gain factor of exactly 1.0 for the missing frames, resulting in a smooth &#8220;fade in&#8221; and &#8220;fade out&#8221; at the beginning and at the end of the input, respectively.</p>
<dt><code>compress, s</code></p>
<dd> Set the compress factor. In range from 0.0 to 30.0. Default is 0.0. By default, the Dynamic Audio Normalizer does not apply &#8220;traditional&#8221; compression. This means that signal peaks will not be pruned and thus the full dynamic range will be retained within each local neighbourhood. However, in some cases it may be desirable to combine the Dynamic Audio Normalizer&#8217;s normalization algorithm with a more &#8220;traditional&#8221; compression. For this purpose, the Dynamic Audio Normalizer provides an optional compression (thresholding) function. If (and only if) the compression feature is enabled, all input frames will be processed by a soft knee thresholding function prior to the actual normalization process. Put simply, the thresholding function is going to prune all samples whose magnitude exceeds a certain threshold value. However, the Dynamic Audio Normalizer does not simply apply a fixed threshold value. Instead, the threshold value will be adjusted for each individual frame. In general, smaller parameters result in stronger compression, and vice versa. Values below 3.0 are not recommended, because audible distortion may appear.</p>
<dt><code>threshold, t</code></p>
<dd> Set the target threshold value. This specifies the lowest permissible magnitude level for the audio input which will be normalized. If input frame volume is above this value frame will be normalized. Otherwise frame may not be normalized at all. The default value is set to 0, which means all input frames will be normalized. This option is mostly useful if digital noise is not wanted to be amplified.</dl>
<p> <i>Commands</i> </p>
<p> This filter supports the all above options as <code>commands</code>.</p>
<h3>earwax</h3>
<p> Make audio easier to listen to on headphones. </p>
<p> This filter adds `cues&#8217; to 44.1kHz stereo (i.e. audio <font SIZE="-1">CD</font> format) audio so that when listened to on headphones the stereo image is moved from inside your head (standard for headphones) to outside and in front of the listener (standard for speakers). </p>
<p> Ported from SoX.</p>
<h3>equalizer</h3>
<p> Apply a two-pole peaking equalisation (<font SIZE="-1">EQ</font>) filter. With this filter, the signal-level at and around a selected frequency can be increased or decreased, whilst (unlike bandpass and bandreject filters) that at all other frequencies is unchanged. </p>
<p> In order to produce complex equalisation curves, this filter can be given several times, each with a different central frequency. </p>
<p> The filter accepts the following options:</p>
<dl>
<dt><code>frequency, f</code></p>
<dd> Set the filter&#8217;s central frequency in Hz.</p>
<dt><code>width_type, t</code></p>
<dd> Set method to specify band-width of filter.</p>
<dl>
<dt>
<dd>
<dl>
<dt><code>h</code></p>
<dd> Hz</p>
<dt><code>q</code></p>
<dd> Q-Factor</p>
<dt><code>o</code></p>
<dd> octave</p>
<dt><code>s</code></p>
<dd> slope</p>
<dt><code>k</code></p>
<dd> kHz</dl>
</dl>
<dl>
<dt>
<dd></dl>
<dt><code>width, w</code></p>
<dd> Specify the band-width of a filter in width_type units.</p>
<dt><code>gain, g</code></p>
<dd> Set the required gain or attenuation in dB. Beware of clipping when using a positive gain.</p>
<dt><code>mix, m</code></p>
<dd> How much to use filtered signal in output. Default is 1. Range is between 0 and 1.</p>
<dt><code>channels, c</code></p>
<dd> Specify which channels to filter, by default all available are filtered.</p>
<dt><code>normalize, n</code></p>
<dd> Normalize biquad coefficients, by default is disabled. Enabling it will normalize magnitude response at <font SIZE="-1">DC</font> to 0dB.</p>
<dt><code>transform, a</code></p>
<dd> Set transform type of <font SIZE="-1">IIR</font> filter.</p>
<dl>
<dt>
<dd>
<dl>
<dt><code>di</code></p>
<dd>
<dt><code>dii</code></p>
<dd>
<dt><code>tdii</code></p>
<dd>
<dt><code>latt</code></p>
<dd></dl>
</dl>
<dl>
<dt>
<dd></dl>
<dt><code>precision, r</code></p>
<dd> Set precison of filtering.</p>
<dl>
<dt>
<dd>
<dl>
<dt><code>auto</code></p>
<dd> Pick automatic sample format depending on surround filters.</p>
<dt><code>s16</code></p>
<dd> Always use signed 16-bit.</p>
<dt><code>s32</code></p>
<dd> Always use signed 32-bit.</p>
<dt><code>f32</code></p>
<dd> Always use float 32-bit.</p>
<dt><code>f64</code></p>
<dd> Always use float 64-bit.</dl>
</dl>
<dl>
<dt>
<dd></dl>
</dl>
<p> <i>Examples</i></p>
<dl>
<dt>&bull;</p>
<dd> Attenuate 10 dB at 1000 Hz, with a bandwidth of 200 Hz: </p>
<pre>
        equalizer=f=1000:t=h:width=200:g=-10
</pre>
<dt>&bull;</p>
<dd> Apply 2 dB gain at 1000 Hz with Q 1 and attenuate 5 dB at 100 Hz with Q 2: </p>
<pre>
        equalizer=f=1000:t=q:w=1:g=2,equalizer=f=100:t=q:w=2:g=-5
</pre>
</dl>
<p> <i>Commands</i> </p>
<p> This filter supports the following commands:</p>
<dl>
<dt><code>frequency, f</code></p>
<dd> Change equalizer frequency. Syntax for the command is : &quot;<i>frequency</i>&quot;</p>
<dt><code>width_type, t</code></p>
<dd> Change equalizer width_type. Syntax for the command is : &quot;<i>width_type</i>&quot;</p>
<dt><code>width, w</code></p>
<dd> Change equalizer width. Syntax for the command is : &quot;<i>width</i>&quot;</p>
<dt><code>gain, g</code></p>
<dd> Change equalizer gain. Syntax for the command is : &quot;<i>gain</i>&quot;</p>
<dt><code>mix, m</code></p>
<dd> Change equalizer mix. Syntax for the command is : &quot;<i>mix</i>&quot;</dl>
<h3>extrastereo</h3>
<p> Linearly increases the difference between left and right channels which adds some sort of &#8220;live&#8221; effect to playback. </p>
<p> The filter accepts the following options:</p>
<dl>
<dt><code>m</code></p>
<dd> Sets the difference coefficient (default: 2.5). 0.0 means mono sound (average of both channels), with 1.0 sound will be unchanged, with -1.0 left and right channels will be swapped.</p>
<dt><code>c</code></p>
<dd> Enable clipping. By default is enabled.</dl>
<p> <i>Commands</i> </p>
<p> This filter supports the all above options as <code>commands</code>.</p>
<h3>firequalizer</h3>
<p> Apply <font SIZE="-1">FIR</font> Equalization using arbitrary frequency response. </p>
<p> The filter accepts the following option:</p>
<dl>
<dt><code>gain</code></p>
<dd> Set gain curve equation (in dB). The expression can contain variables:</p>
<dl>
<dt>
<dd>
<dl>
<dt><code>f</code></p>
<dd> the evaluated frequency</p>
<dt><code>sr</code></p>
<dd> sample rate</p>
<dt><code>ch</code></p>
<dd> channel number, set to 0 when multichannels evaluation is disabled</p>
<dt><code>chid</code></p>
<dd> channel id, see libavutil/channel_layout.h, set to the first channel id when multichannels evaluation is disabled</p>
<dt><code>chs</code></p>
<dd> number of channels</p>
<dt><code>chlayout</code></p>
<dd> channel_layout, see libavutil/channel_layout.h</dl>
</dl>
<dl>
<dt>
<dd>
<p> and functions:</p>
<dl>
<dt><code>gain_interpolate(f)</code></p>
<dd> interpolate gain on frequency f based on gain_entry</p>
<dt><code>cubic_interpolate(f)</code></p>
<dd> same as gain_interpolate, but smoother</dl>
</dl>
<dl>
<dt>
<dd>
<p> This option is also available as command. Default is <tt>gain_interpolate(f)</tt>.</dl>
<dt><code>gain_entry</code></p>
<dd> Set gain entry for gain_interpolate function. The expression can contain functions:</p>
<dl>
<dt>
<dd>
<dl>
<dt><code>entry(f, g)</code></p>
<dd> store gain entry at frequency f with value g</dl>
</dl>
<dl>
<dt>
<dd>
<p> This option is also available as command.</dl>
<dt><code>delay</code></p>
<dd> Set filter delay in seconds. Higher value means more accurate. Default is <tt>0.01</tt>.</p>
<dt><code>accuracy</code></p>
<dd> Set filter accuracy in Hz. Lower value means more accurate. Default is <tt>5</tt>.</p>
<dt><code>wfunc</code></p>
<dd> Set window function. Acceptable values are:</p>
<dl>
<dt>
<dd>
<dl>
<dt><code>rectangular</code></p>
<dd> rectangular window, useful when gain curve is already smooth</p>
<dt><code>hann</code></p>
<dd> hann window (default)</p>
<dt><code>hamming</code></p>
<dd> hamming window</p>
<dt><code>blackman</code></p>
<dd> blackman window</p>
<dt><code>nuttall3</code></p>
<dd> 3-terms continuous 1st derivative nuttall window</p>
<dt><code>mnuttall3</code></p>
<dd> minimum 3-terms discontinuous nuttall window</p>
<dt><code>nuttall</code></p>
<dd> 4-terms continuous 1st derivative nuttall window</p>
<dt><code>bnuttall</code></p>
<dd> minimum 4-terms discontinuous nuttall (blackman-nuttall) window</p>
<dt><code>bharris</code></p>
<dd> blackman-harris window</p>
<dt><code>tukey</code></p>
<dd> tukey window</dl>
</dl>
<dl>
<dt>
<dd></dl>
<dt><code>fixed</code></p>
<dd> If enabled, use fixed number of audio samples. This improves speed when filtering with large delay. Default is disabled.</p>
<dt><code>multi</code></p>
<dd> Enable multichannels evaluation on gain. Default is disabled.</p>
<dt><code>zero_phase</code></p>
<dd> Enable zero phase mode by subtracting timestamp to compensate delay. Default is disabled.</p>
<dt><code>scale</code></p>
<dd> Set scale used by gain. Acceptable values are:</p>
<dl>
<dt>
<dd>
<dl>
<dt><code>linlin</code></p>
<dd> linear frequency, linear gain</p>
<dt><code>linlog</code></p>
<dd> linear frequency, logarithmic (in dB) gain (default)</p>
<dt><code>loglin</code></p>
<dd> logarithmic (in octave scale where 20 Hz is 0) frequency, linear gain</p>
<dt><code>loglog</code></p>
<dd> logarithmic frequency, logarithmic gain</dl>
</dl>
<dl>
<dt>
<dd></dl>
<dt><code>dumpfile</code></p>
<dd> Set file for dumping, suitable for gnuplot.</p>
<dt><code>dumpscale</code></p>
<dd> Set scale for dumpfile. Acceptable values are same with scale option. Default is linlog.</p>
<dt><code>fft2</code></p>
<dd> Enable 2-channel convolution using complex <font SIZE="-1">FFT.</font> This improves speed significantly. Default is disabled.</p>
<dt><code>min_phase</code></p>
<dd> Enable minimum phase impulse response. Default is disabled.</dl>
<p> <i>Examples</i></p>
<dl>
<dt>&bull;</p>
<dd> lowpass at 1000 Hz: </p>
<pre>
        firequalizer=gain='if(lt(f,1000), 0, -INF)'
</pre>
<dt>&bull;</p>
<dd> lowpass at 1000 Hz with gain_entry: </p>
<pre>
        firequalizer=gain_entry='entry(1000,0); entry(1001, -INF)'
</pre>
<dt>&bull;</p>
<dd> custom equalization: </p>
<p>firequalizer = gain_entry = &#8216;entry(100,0); entry(400, -4); entry(1000, -6); entry(2000, 0)&#8217;</p>
<dt>&bull;</p>
<dd> higher delay with zero phase to compensate delay: </p>
<pre>
        firequalizer=delay=0.1:fixed=on:zero_phase=on
</pre>
<dt>&bull;</p>
<dd> lowpass on left channel, highpass on right channel: </p>
<p>firequalizer = gain = &#8216;if(eq(chid,1), gain_interpolate(f), if(eq(chid,2), gain_interpolate(1e6+f), 0))&#8217;<br />
    : gain_entry = &#8216;entry(1000, 0); entry(1001,-INF); entry(1e6+1000,0)&#8217; : multi = on
</dl>
<h3>flanger</h3>
<p> Apply a flanging effect to the audio. </p>
<p> The filter accepts the following options:</p>
<dl>
<dt><code>delay</code></p>
<dd> Set base delay in milliseconds. Range from 0 to 30. Default value is 0.</p>
<dt><code>depth</code></p>
<dd> Set added sweep delay in milliseconds. Range from 0 to 10. Default value is 2.</p>
<dt><code>regen</code></p>
<dd> Set percentage regeneration (delayed signal feedback). Range from -95 to 95. Default value is 0.</p>
<dt><code>width</code></p>
<dd> Set percentage of delayed signal mixed with original. Range from 0 to 100. Default value is 71.</p>
<dt><code>speed</code></p>
<dd> Set sweeps per second (Hz). Range from 0.1 to 10. Default value is 0.5.</p>
<dt><code>shape</code></p>
<dd> Set swept wave shape, can be <i>triangular</i> or <i>sinusoidal</i>. Default value is <i>sinusoidal</i>.</p>
<dt><code>phase</code></p>
<dd> Set swept wave percentage-shift for multi channel. Range from 0 to 100. Default value is 25.</p>
<dt><code>interp</code></p>
<dd> Set delay-line interpolation, <i>linear</i> or <i>quadratic</i>. Default is <i>linear</i>.</dl>
<h3>haas</h3>
<p> Apply Haas effect to audio. </p>
<p> Note that this makes most sense to apply on mono signals. With this filter applied to mono signals it give some directionality and stretches its stereo image. </p>
<p> The filter accepts the following options:</p>
<dl>
<dt><code>level_in</code></p>
<dd> Set input level. By default is <i>1</i>, or 0dB</p>
<dt><code>level_out</code></p>
<dd> Set output level. By default is <i>1</i>, or 0dB.</p>
<dt><code>side_gain</code></p>
<dd> Set gain applied to side part of signal. By default is <i>1</i>.</p>
<dt><code>middle_source</code></p>
<dd> Set kind of middle source. Can be one of the following:</p>
<dl>
<dt>
<dd>
<dl>
<dt><code>left</code></p>
<dd> Pick left channel.</p>
<dt><code>right</code></p>
<dd> Pick right channel.</p>
<dt><code>mid</code></p>
<dd> Pick middle part signal of stereo image.</p>
<dt><code>side</code></p>
<dd> Pick side part signal of stereo image.</dl>
</dl>
<dl>
<dt>
<dd></dl>
<dt><code>middle_phase</code></p>
<dd> Change middle phase. By default is disabled.</p>
<dt><code>left_delay</code></p>
<dd> Set left channel delay. By default is <i>2.05</i> milliseconds.</p>
<dt><code>left_balance</code></p>
<dd> Set left channel balance. By default is <i>-1</i>.</p>
<dt><code>left_gain</code></p>
<dd> Set left channel gain. By default is <i>1</i>.</p>
<dt><code>left_phase</code></p>
<dd> Change left phase. By default is disabled.</p>
<dt><code>right_delay</code></p>
<dd> Set right channel delay. By defaults is <i>2.12</i> milliseconds.</p>
<dt><code>right_balance</code></p>
<dd> Set right channel balance. By default is <i>1</i>.</p>
<dt><code>right_gain</code></p>
<dd> Set right channel gain. By default is <i>1</i>.</p>
<dt><code>right_phase</code></p>
<dd> Change right phase. By default is enabled.</dl>
<h3>hdcd</h3>
<p> Decodes High Definition Compatible Digital (<font SIZE="-1">HDCD</font>) data. A 16-bit <font SIZE="-1">PCM</font> stream with embedded <font SIZE="-1">HDCD</font> codes is expanded into a 20-bit <font SIZE="-1">PCM</font> stream. </p>
<p> The filter supports the Peak Extend and Low-level Gain Adjustment features of <font SIZE="-1">HDCD,</font> and detects the Transient Filter flag. </p>
<pre>
        ffmpeg -i HDCD16.flac -af hdcd OUT24.flac
</pre>
<p> When using the filter with wav, note the default encoding for wav is 16-bit, so the resulting 20-bit stream will be truncated back to 16-bit. Use something like <code>-acodec pcm_s24le</code> after the filter to get 24-bit <font SIZE="-1">PCM</font> output. </p>
<pre>
        ffmpeg -i HDCD16.wav -af hdcd OUT16.wav
        ffmpeg -i HDCD16.wav -af hdcd -c:a pcm_s24le OUT24.wav
</pre>
<p> The filter accepts the following options:</p>
<dl>
<dt><code>disable_autoconvert</code></p>
<dd> Disable any automatic format conversion or resampling in the filter graph.</p>
<dt><code>process_stereo</code></p>
<dd> Process the stereo channels together. If target_gain does not match between channels, consider it invalid and use the last valid target_gain.</p>
<dt><code>cdt_ms</code></p>
<dd> Set the code detect timer period in ms.</p>
<dt><code>force_pe</code></p>
<dd> Always extend peaks above -3dBFS even if <font SIZE="-1">PE</font> isn&#8217;t signaled.</p>
<dt><code>analyze_mode</code></p>
<dd> Replace audio with a solid tone and adjust the amplitude to signal some specific aspect of the decoding process. The output file can be loaded in an audio editor alongside the original to aid analysis. </p>
<p> <tt>&quot;analyze_mode=pe:force_pe=true&quot;</tt> can be used to see all samples above the <font SIZE="-1">PE</font> level. </p>
<p> Modes are:</p>
<dl>
<dt>
<dd>
<dl>
<dt><code>0, off</code></p>
<dd> Disabled</p>
<dt><code>1, lle</code></p>
<dd> Gain adjustment level at each sample</p>
<dt><code>2, pe</code></p>
<dd> Samples where peak extend occurs</p>
<dt><code>3, cdt</code></p>
<dd> Samples where the code detect timer is active</p>
<dt><code>4, tgm</code></p>
<dd> Samples where the target gain does not match between channels</dl>
</dl>
<dl>
<dt>
<dd></dl>
</dl>
<h3>headphone</h3>
<p> Apply head-related transfer functions (HRTFs) to create virtual loudspeakers around the user for binaural listening via headphones. The HRIRs are provided via additional streams, for each channel one stereo input stream is needed. </p>
<p> The filter accepts the following options:</p>
<dl>
<dt><code>map</code></p>
<dd> Set mapping of input streams for convolution. The argument is a &#8216;|&#8217;-separated list of channel names in order as they are given as additional stream inputs for filter. This also specify number of input streams. Number of input streams must be not less than number of channels in first stream plus one.</p>
<dt><code>gain</code></p>
<dd> Set gain applied to audio. Value is in dB. Default is 0.</p>
<dt><code>type</code></p>
<dd> Set processing type. Can be <i>time</i> or <i>freq</i>. <i>time</i> is processing audio in time domain which is slow. <i>freq</i> is processing audio in frequency domain which is fast. Default is <i>freq</i>.</p>
<dt><code>lfe</code></p>
<dd> Set custom gain for <font SIZE="-1">LFE</font> channels. Value is in dB. Default is 0.</p>
<dt><code>size</code></p>
<dd> Set size of frame in number of samples which will be processed at once. Default value is <i>1024</i>. Allowed range is from 1024 to 96000.</p>
<dt><code>hrir</code></p>
<dd> Set format of hrir stream. Default value is <i>stereo</i>. Alternative value is <i>multich</i>. If value is set to <i>stereo</i>, number of additional streams should be greater or equal to number of input channels in first input stream. Also each additional stream should have stereo number of channels. If value is set to <i>multich</i>, number of additional streams should be exactly one. Also number of input channels of additional stream should be equal or greater than twice number of channels of first input stream.</dl>
<p> <i>Examples</i></p>
<dl>
<dt>&bull;</p>
<dd> Full example using wav files as coefficients with amovie filters for 7.1 downmix, each amovie filter use stereo file with <font SIZE="-1">IR</font> coefficients as input. The files give coefficients for each position of virtual loudspeaker: </p>
<p>ffmpeg &#8211; i input.wav &#8211; filter_complex &#8220;amovie=azi_270_ele_0_DFC.wav[sr];amovie=azi_90_ele_0_DFC.wav[sl];amovie=azi_225_ele_0_DFC.wav[br];amovie=azi_135_ele_0_DFC.wav[bl];amovie=azi_0_ele_0_DFC.wav,asplit[fc][lfe];amovie=azi_35_ele_0_DFC.wav[fl];amovie=azi_325_ele_0_DFC.wav[fr];[0:a][fl][fr][fc][lfe][bl][br][sl][sr]headphone=FL|FR|FC|LFE|BL|BR|SL|SR&#8221; output.wav</p>
<dt>&bull;</p>
<dd> Full example using wav files as coefficients with amovie filters for 7.1 downmix, but now in <i>multich</i> <i>hrir</i> format. </p>
<p>ffmpeg &#8211; i input.wav &#8211; filter_complex &#8220;amovie=minp.wav[hrirs];[0:a][hrirs]headphone=map=FL|FR|FC|LFE|BL|BR|SL|SR:hrir=multich&#8221; output.wav
</dl>
<h3>highpass</h3>
<p> Apply a high-pass filter with 3dB point frequency. The filter can be either single-pole, or double-pole (the default). The filter roll off at 6dB per pole per octave (20dB per pole per decade). </p>
<p> The filter accepts the following options:</p>
<dl>
<dt><code>frequency, f</code></p>
<dd> Set frequency in Hz. Default is 3000.</p>
<dt><code>poles, p</code></p>
<dd> Set number of poles. Default is 2.</p>
<dt><code>width_type, t</code></p>
<dd> Set method to specify band-width of filter.</p>
<dl>
<dt>
<dd>
<dl>
<dt><code>h</code></p>
<dd> Hz</p>
<dt><code>q</code></p>
<dd> Q-Factor</p>
<dt><code>o</code></p>
<dd> octave</p>
<dt><code>s</code></p>
<dd> slope</p>
<dt><code>k</code></p>
<dd> kHz</dl>
</dl>
<dl>
<dt>
<dd></dl>
<dt><code>width, w</code></p>
<dd> Specify the band-width of a filter in width_type units. Applies only to double-pole filter. The default is 0.707q and gives a Butterworth response.</p>
<dt><code>mix, m</code></p>
<dd> How much to use filtered signal in output. Default is 1. Range is between 0 and 1.</p>
<dt><code>channels, c</code></p>
<dd> Specify which channels to filter, by default all available are filtered.</p>
<dt><code>normalize, n</code></p>
<dd> Normalize biquad coefficients, by default is disabled. Enabling it will normalize magnitude response at <font SIZE="-1">DC</font> to 0dB.</p>
<dt><code>transform, a</code></p>
<dd> Set transform type of <font SIZE="-1">IIR</font> filter.</p>
<dl>
<dt>
<dd>
<dl>
<dt><code>di</code></p>
<dd>
<dt><code>dii</code></p>
<dd>
<dt><code>tdii</code></p>
<dd>
<dt><code>latt</code></p>
<dd></dl>
</dl>
<dl>
<dt>
<dd></dl>
<dt><code>precision, r</code></p>
<dd> Set precison of filtering.</p>
<dl>
<dt>
<dd>
<dl>
<dt><code>auto</code></p>
<dd> Pick automatic sample format depending on surround filters.</p>
<dt><code>s16</code></p>
<dd> Always use signed 16-bit.</p>
<dt><code>s32</code></p>
<dd> Always use signed 32-bit.</p>
<dt><code>f32</code></p>
<dd> Always use float 32-bit.</p>
<dt><code>f64</code></p>
<dd> Always use float 64-bit.</dl>
</dl>
<dl>
<dt>
<dd></dl>
</dl>
<p> <i>Commands</i> </p>
<p> This filter supports the following commands:</p>
<dl>
<dt><code>frequency, f</code></p>
<dd> Change highpass frequency. Syntax for the command is : &quot;<i>frequency</i>&quot;</p>
<dt><code>width_type, t</code></p>
<dd> Change highpass width_type. Syntax for the command is : &quot;<i>width_type</i>&quot;</p>
<dt><code>width, w</code></p>
<dd> Change highpass width. Syntax for the command is : &quot;<i>width</i>&quot;</p>
<dt><code>mix, m</code></p>
<dd> Change highpass mix. Syntax for the command is : &quot;<i>mix</i>&quot;</dl>
<h3>join</h3>
<p> Join multiple input streams into one multi-channel stream. </p>
<p> It accepts the following parameters:</p>
<dl>
<dt><code>inputs</code></p>
<dd> The number of input streams. It defaults to 2.</p>
<dt><code>channel_layout</code></p>
<dd> The desired output channel layout. It defaults to stereo.</p>
<dt><code>map</code></p>
<dd> Map channels from inputs to output. The argument is a &#8216;|&#8217;-separated list of mappings, each in the <tt>&quot;</tt>input_idx<tt>.</tt>in_channel<tt>-</tt>out_channel<tt>&quot;</tt> form. <i>input_idx</i> is the 0-based index of the input stream. <i>in_channel</i> can be either the name of the input channel (e.g. <font SIZE="-1">FL</font> for front left) or its index in the specified input stream. <i>out_channel</i> is the name of the output channel.</dl>
<p> The filter will attempt to guess the mappings when they are not specified explicitly. It does so by first trying to find an unused matching input channel and if that fails it picks the first unused input channel. </p>
<p> Join 3 inputs (with properly set channel layouts): </p>
<pre>
        ffmpeg -i INPUT1 -i INPUT2 -i INPUT3 -filter_complex join=inputs=3 OUTPUT
</pre>
<p> Build a 5.1 output from 6 single-channel streams: </p>
<pre>
        ffmpeg -i fl -i fr -i fc -i sl -i sr -i lfe -filter_complex
        'join=inputs=6:channel_layout=5.1:map=0.0-FL|1.0-FR|2.0-FC|3.0-SL|4.0-SR|5.0-LFE'
        out
</pre>
<h3>ladspa</h3>
<p> Load a <font SIZE="-1">LADSPA</font> (Linux Audio Developer&#8217;s Simple Plugin <font SIZE="-1">API</font>) plugin. </p>
<p> To enable compilation of this filter you need to configure FFmpeg with <tt>&quot;--enable-ladspa&quot;</tt>.</p>
<dl>
<dt><code>file, f</code></p>
<dd> Specifies the name of <font SIZE="-1">LADSPA</font> plugin library to load. If the environment variable <font SIZE="-1"><code>LADSPA_PATH</code></font> is defined, the <font SIZE="-1">LADSPA</font> plugin is searched in each one of the directories specified by the colon separated list in <font SIZE="-1"><code>LADSPA_PATH</code></font>, otherwise in the standard <font SIZE="-1">LADSPA</font> paths, which are in this order: <i></i><font SIZE="-1"><i>HOME/</i></font><i>.ladspa/lib/</i>, <i>/usr/local/lib/ladspa/</i>, <i>/usr/lib/ladspa/</i>.</p>
<dt><code>plugin, p</code></p>
